[[P723]]CHAPTER XVI

[[LIST OF TĪRTHÁS|LIST OF TĪRTHAS]]

This is merely a list of [[tirthas|tīrthas]] from the point of view of Dharmaśāstra and not a treatise on the Ancient Geography of India. The result is that many countries and towns that have no importance or claims as [[tirthas|tīrthas]] have been altogether omitted. Bauddha and Jain works on [[tirthas|tīrthas]] have only rarely been referred to. Many of the [[Purānas|Purāṇas]] enumerate the [[moun tains|mountains]] and rivers of [[dvipas|dvīpas]] and [[var ṣas|varṣas]] other than those of [[Jambudvipa|Jambudvīpa]] and [[Bhārata-varsa|Bhārata-varṣa]] such as Harivarṣa, [[Ramyaka varsa|Ramyaka-varṣa]], [[Krauñcadvipa|Krauñcadvīpa]], [[Sālmalidvipa|Śālmalidvīpa]], but they have been [[exclud ed|excluded]] from this list. The Brahmapurāṇa (chap. 26 verses 8-83) contains a string of over 520 [[tirthas|tīrthas]] without hardly any certain indication of their location and the Bhīṣmaparva in chap. 9 mentions about 160 rivers with practically no indication about their location in most cases. Similarly, the [[Garuda|Garuḍa]] (I. 81. 1-31) enumerates about 200 and Padma (VI. 129) contains 108 [[tirtha|tīrtha]] names. In Banaras alone I have listed about 350 [[sub tirthas|sub-tīrthas]]. But there are about 1500 shrines and temples in Banaras alone. These have not been included. Each great [[tirtha|tīrtha]] has numerous sub-tīrthas as in the Varāha-purāṇa under Mathurā, in Brahma-purāṇa under [[Gautami|Gautamī]], in Vāyu-purāṇa under [[Gaya|Gayā]]. Through sheer weariness or inadvertence some of these must have been left out by me and some were left out purposely on account of the absence of any importance. Besides, several [[tirthas|tīrthas]] that are regarded as sacred by modern people are not so treated in the Epics or purāṇas and are not even mentioned in them; many of these have been omitted in this list. Our authorities are often very vague about the location or extent of [[tirthas|tīrthas]]. Moreover, a number of [[tirthas|tīrthas]] scattered in different parts of India bear the same name (e. g. vide under [[Agnitirtha|Agnitīrtha]], [[Koțitirtha|Koṭitīrtha]], Cakratīrtha, [[Varāhatirtha|Varāhatīrtha]], [[Somatirtha|Somatīrtha]]). I am under great obligations to Cunningham’s ‘Ancient Geography of India’ [[(ed, of 1872)|(ed. of 1872)]] and to Nundo Lal Dey’s ‘The Geographical Dictionary of Ancient and Medieval India’ (1927). I have had occasions to differ from both, particularly from the latter. But, the space at my disposal being very limited, I could not enter into even brief discussions

[[P724]]in support of my views. It would look ungrateful to find fault with the work of Dey; but it must be said that he relies too much on rather old and antiquated authorities and has not cast his net over a wide area. Often times he mentions no original authorities (vide, for example, [[Cakratirtha|Cakratīrtha]] on p. 43). He is very vague in his references. He hardly ever cites the number of the verse on which he relies and often omits to specify even the part or section of the work relied upon. For example, at p. 13 as regards Avanti he mentions [[Panini|Pāṇini]] IV. 176, which should be IV. 1. 176. Further, he has omitted several well-known [[tirthas|tīrthas]] (e. g. Daśāśvamedhika). He is sometimes wrong in what he says. On p. 54 he states that [[Kiskindha kānda|Kiṣkindhā-kāṇḍa]] 43 refers to Devadāruvana as a proper name, but there the context (’lodhrapadmaka-[[sandesu|ṣaṇḍeṣu]] [[devadāruvanesu|devadāruvaneṣu]] ca’ in verse 15) shows that what is meant is ‘forests of Deodar trees’). On p. 73 he says [[Bharatavarṣa|Bhāratavarṣa]] was first called [[Haimavata varsa|Haimavata-varṣa]], but the passage of the [[Lingapurāṇa|Liṅgapurāṇa]] [[(I. 49.7)|(I. 49. 7)]] on which he relies (idam [[haimavatam|haimavataṁ]] [[varsam|varṣaṁ]] [[Bhāratam|Bhārataṁ]] nāma [[visrutam|viśrutam]]) does not bear that out. There is no reference to a prior account here and all that is meant is that [[Bhāratavarsa|Bhāratavarṣa]] is connected with Himavat, as a previous passage (I. 47. 23 [[Himādrer dakṣiṇam varṣam Bharatāya nyavédayat|Himādrer-dakṣiṇaṁ varṣaṁ Bharatāya nyavedayat]]) expressly states that [[Bhāratávarsa|Bhāratavarṣa]] is to the south of Himālaya. In [[Salya 44.52|Śalya 44. 52]] [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] is called [[Haimavati|Haimavatī]], but that does not mean that [[Haimavati|Haimavatī]] was the name of [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] in far-off ages. It simply means that it [[‘rises in Himayat|‘rises in Himavat]] range.’ Only a comparison of the list prepared by me with Dey’s work will show the [[differ ence|difference]] in details between the two. I should not undertake that task and leave it to scholarly readers.

I have carefully read the two Epics and most of the [[Purānas|Purāṇas]]. But I do not cite exhaustive references to all the texts where a particular [[tirtha|tīrtha]] is mentioned. I feel satisfied with mentioning two or three. Owing to the several [[restric tions|restrictions]] imposed upon myself this list cannot claim to be thoroughly exhaustive. But I hope that it is comprehensive enough and far larger and more informative than any list of [[tirthas|tīrthas]] presented by any scholar so far. There is ample scope for industrious scholars to improve upon and to add to this [[lista have|list. I have]] included important [[tirthas|tīrthas]] from Kashmir and given copious references to the [[Nilamata-purāṇa|Nīlamata-purāṇa]], the [[Rāja-tarangiṇi|Rāja-taraṅgiṇī]] and the [[si Hara-carita-cintāmaṇi|Hara-carita-cintāmaṇi]]. I am highly obliged to the Kashmir Report of Dr. [[Būhler|Bühler]] (published as a special number for [[187 Cient|1877]]

[[P725]]by the B B R A S), to Stein’s notes in his translation of the [[Rāja-tarangini|Rāja-taraṅgiṇī]] and his memoir on the Ancient Geography of Kashmir published separately (and also embodied in the 2nd volume of the translation of [[Kalhana’s|Kalhaṇa’s]] work). All the names of [[tirthas|tīrthas]] are transliterated into the English alphabet and arranged according to the English mode of spelling. For the Mahābhārata references are given to the oblong Bombay edition and by citing names of parvans, but in the case of the [[Rāmāyana|Rāmāyaṇa]] Roman figures I to VII are employed for the Bāla, Ayodhyā, [[Aranya|Āraṇya]], [[Kiskindhā|Kiṣkindhā]], Sundara, Yuddha and Uttara [[kāndas|kāṇḍas]] respectively. For the Rāmāyaṇa I have used the edition brought out by the Madras Law Journal Press in 1933. Among [[Purānas|Purāṇas]] the [[Anandāśrama|Ānandāśrama]] editions of the Agni, Brahma, Brahmavaivarta, Matsya, Vāyu and Padma have been relied upon and as to the rest of the Mahāpurāṇas the [[Venkate śvara|Veṅkaṭe-śvara]] Press editions have been used (except in the case of the [[Nrasimhapurāṇa|Nṛsiṁhapurāṇa]] of which the edition of Messrs. Gopal Narayan & Co. and the Bhāgavatapurāṇa, of which the [[Nirnayasagara|Nirṇayasāgara]] edition of the bare text have been referred to). The Skandapurāṇa has been a source of great trouble and labour. Owing to the limited time at my disposal I have not been able to digest thoroughly the ninety thousand and odd verses of that purāṇa, though I have very carefully gone into the [[Kāśikhanda|Kāśīkhaṇḍa]] and some other [[khandas|khaṇḍas]]. Besides, there are two separate recensions of the Skanda and large portions of it appear to be apocryphal and later additions. The seven big sections of the Skanda viz. [[Maheśvarakhanda|Māheśvarakhaṇḍa]], [[Vaiṣpava|Vaiṣṇava]], Brāhma, [[Kāśi|Kāśī]], [[Avantya|Āvantya]], Nāgara, Prabhāsa are indicated by Roman figures from I to VII and the sub-sections by Arabic figures. Some of these sub-sections have further sub-divisions into Pūrvārdha and Uttarārdha.

[[BUD on I have|I have]] made endeavours to locate the [[tirthas|tīrthas]] as far as possible. Inscriptions have been drawn upon in several cases for locating the [[tirthas|tīrthas]] and for their antiquities and history. The principal Sanskrit authorities (except [[Kalhana|Kalhaṇa]]) are often very vague as compared with even foreign writers like Hiouen Thsang, Alberuni and Abul Fazal. Where I was not myself sure I have given references to the texts alone, and in some cases to the views of Cunningham, Dey, [[Pargiterband|Pargiter and]] others. If the same name happens to be given to different [[firth as|tīrthas]] located at different places, I have indicated the several different places bearing the same name by employing the capital letters

[[P726]]A, B, C and so on. I may state that I have hardly ever remained content by borrowing references to Sanskrit authorities given by my predecessors, but have seen personally each reference to Sanskrit works except where I expressly state from whom I borrow a particular reference. Sorensen’s Index of the Mahābhārata and the Vedic Index of Macdonell and Keith have been used in several places. I have also derived material help from the volumes of the Imperial Gazetteer and from the several [[volunies|volumes]] of the Bombay Gazetteer. The same may be said about Wilson’s translation of [[Visnupurāṇa|Viṣṇupurāṇa]] (ed. by Hall, 1864–1877), Pargiter’s notes to his translation of the [[Mārkan. deyapurāṇa|Mārkaṇḍeyapurāṇa]], Dr. B. C. Law’s paper on the ‘mountains and rivers of India’ in the Journal of the Department of Letters, Calcutta University, vol. 28 and Dr. Hemchandra [[Raychaud huri’s|Raychaudhuri’s]] ‘Studies in Indian Antiquities’ (1932), and the paper on ‘Ancient towns and cities in Gujarat and Kathiawad’ in I. A. vol. 54. I have consulted only a few Māhātmyas of [[tirthas|tīrthas]]. Recently, Prof. V. R. Ramchandra Dikshitar of Madras has published a useful work ‘The Purāṇa Index’ (volumes I and II covering over 1400 pages) which collects material from five [[Purānas|Purāṇas]] only viz. the Bhāgavata, [[Brahmānda|Brahmāṇḍa]], Matsya, Vāyu and [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]]. The two volumes reach up to the Sanskrit letter ‘ma’ only. I, for my part, do not agree with much that he says about the age of the [[Purānas|Purāṇas]] in general and of the five [[Purānas|Purāṇas]] (which he has digested) in particular, but this is not the place to go into that question. Besides, his work has to be used with some caution. In several places the work is misleading. To take only a [[fe|few]] examples. On p. 547 he lists ‘Godhūma’, makes the remark ‘fit for śrāddha’ and cites four references from the Viṣṇupurāṇa alone. One may prima facie think that Godhūma is here some place fit for śrāddha. As a matter of fact in all the references from the Viṣṇupurāṇa ‘Godhūma’ means ‘wheat’ and nothing more, which no doubt is fit food for śrāddha. But other fit things such as ‘ikṣu’ (declared to be fit for [[sraddha|śrāddha]] by Vāyu [[78. -8)|78. 7-8)]] are not included at all. On p. 123 [[A.śokavana|Aśokavana]] is not a proper name, but only a forest of [[Asoka|Aśoka]] trees, as the preceding word [[‘drāksāvana’|‘drākṣāvana’]] (in Vāyu 38. 68) indicates. So also [[Kamala prabhava|Kamala-prabhava]] (not ‘prabhavā’ as printed on p. 316 of vol. I) is not a river (in Matsya 163. 62, [[Anandaśrama|Ānandāśrama]] ed.) but is only [[sus adjective|an adjective]] of the word [[Sona|Śoṇa]] that follows. On p. 316 of vol. II [[Hobb states|he states]] that the river Pārā rises from [[Rsyavān|Ṛṣyavān]], but the [[Matsya round 114, 24|Matsya 114. 24]] on which he relies says it springs from Pāriyātra. On the same page he gives the entry [[“Pārāvāratata|‘Pārāvārataṭa]] sacred to

[[P727]][[Mata’.|Mātā’.]] This makes no sense. [[Pārāvāratata|Pārāvārataṭa]] would mean ’the shore of the sea’. What is really meant is that [[Devi|Devī]] is styled or worshipped as Pārā on the bank of the Pārā river ([[Pārā Pārātate mata).|Pārā Pārātaṭe matā).]] He does not compare the readings of the several printed editions of the five [[pūrānas.|purāṇas.]] Besides, the information furnished is very meagre. If the whole had been printed in a compact form far more information could have been given in the same number of pages.

In some places I have indicated the rewards promised for bathing in the [[tirthas;|tīrthas;]] but I have not done so in every case. The purāṇas promise one or more of the following rewards viz. the same merit as the performance of Aśvamedha or Vājapeya or other solemn Vedic sacrifices or reaching the highest worlds such as those of Brahmā, [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] or [[Siva,|Śiva,]] the destruction of the gravest sins such as brāhmaṇa murder, the same merit as arises from gifts of a thousand cows &c.

Many of the doubts and difficulties about the identification of several tīrthas can, if at all, be solved only by means of actual journeys to various places and investigations on the spot. That is a task which would require for its proper [[accom plishment|accomplishment]] a team of workers. A single individual can hardly attempt such a task with his own resources.

[[ul This list|This list]] will, it is hoped, be of some use in elucidating several problems such as the questions about the relative [[anti quity|antiquity]] of the purāṇas, the dates of several Sanskrit works which mention the [[tirthas|tīrthas]] and the questions about borrowing on the part of purāṇas among themselves and from the Mahābhārata.

It is also a place of pilgrimage for Jains, as some of their saints were born there. Atharvaveda X. 2. 31 and [[Tai, A. 1.|Tai. A. 1.]] 27.2 ([[astācakrā|aṣṭācakrā]] nava [[dvārā devānām pūr-Ayodhyāl|dvārā devānāṁ pūr-Ayodhyā |]] [[tasyām hiranyayah kośab|tasyāṁ hiraṇyayaḥ kośaḥ]] svargo loko [[Jyoti sāvrtah ),|Jyotiṣāvṛtaḥ ||),]] V 60. 24-25 and 70. 2 (capital of king [[Rtuparna|Ṛtuparṇa]] and of Rāma), Br. IV. 40. 91, Ag. 109. 24 ([[Ayodhyā pāpanāśani).|Ayodhyā pāpanāśanī).]] According to [[Rām. 1. 5. 5-7|Rām. I. 5. 5-7]] the country of Kosala had Sarayū flowing through it; Ayodhyā, 12 yojanas long and three broad, was Kosala capital founded by Manu. Kosala was one of the 16 [[mahajanapadas|mahājanapadas]] of India in ancient times (vide [[Anguttara|Aṅguttara]] Nikāya, vol. IV. p. 252). Later on, Kosala was divided into two, viz. Uttara Kosala and [[Daksina|Dakṣiṇa]] Kosala divided by the Sarjū or [[Ghāgrā river, The Raghu vamsa|Ghāgrā river, The Raghuvaṁśa]] holds Ayodhyā to be capital of Uttarakosala (VI. 71 and IX. 1). Vide also Vā 88. 20 ff. for a long line of kings of Ayodhyā from [[Iksvāku|Ikṣvāku]] and P. VI. 208. 46-47 (for [[Dakṣina Kosala|Dakṣiṇa Kosalā]] and [[Uttara Kosala). Saketa|Uttara Kosalā). Sāketa]] is generally identified with Ayodhyā. Vide [[T.P.|T. P.]] p. 496 (gives its boundaries from SK) and under [[Saketa.|Sāketa.]] Dr. B. C. [[Dasarna|Daśārṇa]] is a word meaning a country having ten forts or ‘a river ([[Daśārnā)|Daśārṇā)]] having ten [[waters.|waters.’]] The Vārtikas are [[‘pra-vat satara-kambalavasanānām carne’|‘pra-vat-satara-kambalavasanānāṁ carṇe’]] and [[‘rnadasabhyam ca’.|‘ṛṇadaśābhyāṁ ca’.]] [[Meghaduta|Meghadūta]] I. [[23-24|23–24]] show that the capital of the [[Dasārna|Daśārṇa]] country was Vidiśā (modern Bhilsā) and the [[Vetravati|Vetravatī]] (Betwa river) was near it. It is the Dosaron of Ptolemy (p. 71). Anu. 25. 21 and 165. 19, K. II. 37. 25, P. I. 25. [[9-14,|9–14,]] N. II. 47. 27, Vi. II. 15. 6, Vām. 78. [[37-all|37—all]] these extol [[Devika,|Devikā,]] but it is not clear what river is meant. NM [[152 153|152–153]] say that it is holy like [[Irāvati,|Irāvatī,]] is identified with Umā and located in Madra i.e. between [[Rāviand|Rāvī and]] Chenab. [[Pan. (VII.3.1)|Pāṇ. (VII. 3. 1)]] expressly mentions the river Devikā and the Mbh. thereon gives the illustration [[‘Dāvikakulah sālayah’|‘Dāvikākūlāḥ śālayaḥ’]] (paddy grown on the banks of the [[Devika|Devikā]] is so called). [[Panini|Pāṇini]] probably mentions some river in the Panjab. Dey p. 55 says that the southern portion of Sarayū is called [[Devika|Devikā]] or Devā. Vām. 84. 12 refers to [[Devikatirtha|Devikātīrtha]] [[brāhmanas,|brāhmaṇas.]] SK. VII [[Prabhasa-mahatmya,|Prabhāsa-māhātmya,]] chap. 278. [[66 67|66–67]] speak of [[Mūlasthana|Mūlasthāna]] (modern Multan) as situated on the [[Devika :|Devikā;]] P. I, 25. [[9-14|9–14]] (5 yojanas long and [[yojana|½ yojana]] wide). In Vi. II. 15. 6 the city called [[Viranagara|Vīranagara]] is said to be situated on the bank of the [[Devika|Devikā]] and as founded by Pulastya. [[Devika|Devikā]] is not Sarayū, as in the Anu. 165. 19 and 21 they two are separately named. [[Br.|Bṛ.]] S. 11. 35 says that a certain malignant Ketu would strike down even [[Devika|Devikā]] in the north. Pargiter (tr. of [[Mar,|Mār.]] p. 292) identified it with the river Deeg or Degh in the Punjab, Dr. [[V. S. Agtawala|V. S. Agrawala]] with the Vular lake in Kashmir ([[J.U.P.H. S.|J. U. P. H. S.]] vol. 16 pp. [[21-22)|21–22)]] and Mr. [[Jagannātha (in J. U. P.H.S.|Jagannātha (in J. U. P. H. S.]] vol. 17 part 2 p. 78 supports Pargiter. With respect I agree with Mr. [[Jagannatha.|Jagannātha.]]

[[Dhruvatirtha-(under|Dhruvatīrtha—(under]] [[Mathura)|Mathurā)]] Var. 152. 58 and 180. 1

[[Drsadvati|Dṛṣadvatī]] (r.). Vide p. 682 above. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] [[III.23.4|III. 23. 4]] it is mentioned along with Āpayā and [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] as a holy river for the worship of Agni. V. 90. 11, Manu II. 17 (calls it [[devanadi),|devanadī),]] N. II. 60. 30, Bh. V. 19. 18. It is identified by some with the [[haggar|Ghaggar]] and by others with

the Chittang (Cambridge History of India, vol. I. p. 80). No trace of the river appears in any modern name. Cunningham’s identification of it with [[Rākshi|Rākshī]] river 17 miles to the south of Thānesar has much to recommend it (A. S. of India, vol. XIV. p. 88).

[[Durgā -(acc.|Durgā—(acc.]] to Bār. S. III. 128 Durgā dwells on Vindhya).

[[Durga-sabhramatisangama-P.|Durgā-sābhramatīsaṅgama—P.]] VI. 169. 1.

[[Durga.(t.|Durgā—(r.]] rising from [[Vindhya )|Vindhya)]] [[Va|Vā]] 45. 103, Br. II. 16. 33.

[[Durgātirtha-A|Durgātīrtha—A]] (under [[Sarasvati)|Sarasvatī)]] Vām. [[42. 14-15:|42. 14–15;]] B (under Go.) B. [[132.8.|132. 8.]]

[[Dvādaśāditya-kunda|Dvādaśāditya-kuṇḍa]] (under [[Badari)|Badarī)]] Var. 141. 24

[[Dvaita-vana-Sat.|Dvaita-vana—Śat.]] Br. XIII. 5. 4. 9 (Dvaita lake named after king Dvaitavana of the Matsyas); V. 11. 68, 24. 10 (commentary gives a fantastic etymology), 237. 13 (it had a lake), [[Sal.|Śal.]] 37. 27 (visited by Balarāma on [[Sarasvati),|Sarasvatī),]] Vām. 22. 12, [[47.56|47. 56]] (it was near [[Sannibatya|Sannihatya]] pool).

[[Dvārakā-A-the|Dvārakā—A—the]] name of this holy city does not occur in the Vedic Literature but the references to it in the Mahābhārata and the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] are plentiful. It is one of the seven [[boly|holy]] cities. Vide p. 678. It appears that there were two Dvārakās, one more ancient than the other. The ancient [[Dvārakā,|Dvārakā]] was situated near [[Kodidara|Kodinar.]] A little mound which rises on the sea-shore between the mouths of the rivers Somat [[apd|and]] [[Sipgavra|Siṅgāvṛt]]

[[P749]]three miles from Kodinar is [[sur rounded|surrounded]] by the ruins of a temple which popular Hindu belief declares to be the original Dvārakā, where [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] resided and whence transferred himself to [[Dvāraka|Dvārakā]] in [[Okhamandala.|Okhamanḍala.]] Vide Bombay Gazetteer, vol. VIII. (on Kathiawar) pp. [[518-520|518–520]] for Kodinar and [[p.552|p. 552]] for [[Mula|Mūla]] Dvārakā. It was founded by [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] (with [[Rai vataka|Raivataka]] as a park and Gomanta as a hill) owing to constant invasions and harassment by [[Jarāsandba;|Jarāsandha;]] it was two yojanas long and one yojana broad. Vide Sabhāparva [[14.49-55.|14. 49–55.]] The Var. [[(149, 7-8)|(149. 7–8)]] makes it 10 [[yoja nas|yojanas]] long and 5 broad. Vide p. 688 above under Mathurā. B [[(14. 54-56)|(14. 54–56)]] says that the [[Vrsnis|Vṛṣṇis]] and Andhakas left Mathurā through fear of [[Kālay avana,|Kālayavana,]] took counsel with [[Krsna,|Kṛṣṇa,]] ran to [[Kusasthali|Kuśasthalī]] and built up Dvārakā: Vi. V. 23. [[13-15.|13–15.]] B 196. [[13-15|13–15]] say that [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] begged of the ocean a strip of twelve yojanas, built [[Dvāraka|Dvārakā]] with large parks, mansions and strong walls and established the people of [[Mathura|Mathurā]] there. When [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] passed away, the city was flooded by the sea and swept [[away.|away,]] as stated in a prophetic vein by Mausala-parva [[6.23-24|6. 23–24]] and 7. [[41-42.|41–42,]] B [[210.55|210. 55]] and [[212.9.|212. 9.]] Vide also Vi. [[V.38.9|V. 38. 9]] (the whole of Dvārakā except [[Krsna’s|Kṛṣṇa’s]] palace was swept by the sea) and BV IV. [[129.44|129. 44]] (except [[Rukmi. ni’s|Rukmiṇī’s]] palace). It is called the capital of [[Anarta|Ānarta]] (Udyoga 7. 6) and was first called [[Kusasthali|Kuśasthalī]] (Sabhā 14. 50). Vide M [[69.9=P.|69. 9 = P.]] V. [[23.10,|23. 10,]] B. [[7.29 32|7. 29–32]] and Ag. 273. 12 ([[Kusasthali|Kuśasthalī]] being the earlier name of the capital). The present [[Dvāraka|Dvārakā]] is near [[Okha|Okhā]] in Kathiawar. The [[Harivamsa|Harivaṁśa]] II ([[Visnu parva)|Viṣṇuparva)]] chapters 58 and 98 deal with the founding of Dvārakā. Some [[an cient|ancient]] Jain works like the [[Uttaradhy ayanasutra|Uttarādhyayanasūtra]] (SBE. vol. 45 [[p.115)|p. 115)]] [[men tion|mention]] Dvārakā and Raivataka park

(Girnar). The [[Jatakas|Jātakas]] also refer to Dvārakā. Vide Dr. B. C. Law’s work on ‘India as described in early texts of Buddhism and Jainism’ pp. [[102,239.|102, 239.]] The [[Prabhāsakhanda|Prabhāsakhaṇḍa]] of the SK has a sub-section on [[Dvāraka|Dvārakā]] in 44 [[chap ters|chapters]] and over 2000 verses for the glorification of Dvārakā. It says [[“the|‘the]] reward that is secured by (pilgrimage to) [[Vārānasi,|Vārāṇasī,]] [[Kuruksetra|Kurukṣetra]] and [[Narmada|Narmadā]] can be secured at [[Dvāraka|Dvārakā]] in half a twinkle (4. [[52)”:|52)’;]] [[“Pilgri mage|‘Pilgrimage]] to [[Dvarakā|Dvārakā]] is the 4th means of mukti. Man secures mukti by acquiring correct knowledge about Brahman or by dying at [[Prayaga|Prayāga]] or by mere bath in [[Gomati|Gomatī]] near [[Krsna”|Kṛṣṇa’]] (SK. VII. 4. 4. [[97-98).|97–98).]] The BV. ([[Krsnajanmakhanda,|Kṛṣṇajanmakhaṇḍa,]] [[Uttarardha|Uttarārdha]] chap. 103) has a hyperbolical [[descrip tion|description]] of the creation of [[Dvaraka,|Dvārakā,]] which is said to have been one hundred yojanas in extent. There is a work called [[Dvāraka-pattalaka|Dvārakā-pattalaka]] compiled by [[Binābāyi|Binābāyī]] (the only [[Ms,|Ms.]] of which is the one at [[B.O.R.I.|B. O. R. I.]] in Poona) which has been published by Dr. J. B. Chaudhuri (1940). The Ms. is dated [[samvat|saṁvat]] 1574 (1518 A. D). It summarises the [[Dvāraka māhātmya|Dvārakā-māhātmya]] in SK. A pilgrim on [[reach ing|reaching]] Dvārakā first worships [[Ganesa,|Gaṇeśa,]] then [[Balarama|Balarāma]] and then [[Krsna;|Kṛṣṇa;]] he visits [[Rukmini’s|Rukmiṇī’s]] temple on the 8th, 9th or 14th tithi, then visits [[Cakra tirtha,|Cakratīrtha,]] then [[Dvārakā Gangā,|Dvārakā Gaṅgā,]] then [[Sankhoddbāra,|Śaṅkhoddhāra,]] then [[tathes|bathes]] in the [[Gomati.|Gomatī.]] The temple of [[Dvāraka natha|Dvārakānātha]] is on the north bank of the [[Gomati|Gomatī]] creek. The main temple has five stories, is about 100 feet high from the ground and is [[sur mounted|surmounted]] by a conical spire rising to about 150 feet. Vide [[D . D.|Dr. A. D.]] Pusalkar’s paper in [[that DOP) C.|the Dr. B. C.]] Law presentation volume [[dopp!|I. pp.]] 218 ff. for further information on [[Pvāraka;|Dvārakā;]]

[[P750]]B (there is [[Dvīrakā|Dvārakā]] in Indraprastha) P. VI. 202. 4 and 62

[[Dvārakā-Krsnatirtha-M.|Dvārakā-Kṛṣṇatīrtha—M.]] 22. 39.

[[Dvāravati-Same|Dvāravatī—Same]] as [[Dvāraka.|Dvārakā.]] It contained the temple of [[Nagesa,|Nāgeśa,]] one of the twelve [[Jyotirlingas.|Jyotirliṅgas.]] The [[Kāsīkbanda|Kāśīkhaṇḍa]] (7. [[104-105 )|104–105)]] states: ‘since in this city there are entrance gates for all the four [[varnas|varṇas]] it was named [[Dvāravati|Dvāravatī]] by the learned. Where even the bones of beings are marked with the sign of the wheel [[(discus).|(discus),]] what wonder is there if the hands (of [[men) are marked 91 with the figures of a conch or wheel ?’|men) are marked with the figures of a conch or wheel ?’]] In a [[Dvaraka-mahatmya|Dvārakā-māhātmya]] stated to be taken from the [[Skanda purana|Skandapurāṇa]] (D. C. Ms. No. 49 of [[82-83,|82–83,]] copied in [[samvat|saṁvat]] 1529 i. e. [[1472-73|1472–73]] A. D.) it is stated that [[Mathurā, Kāsī and Avanti|Mathurā, Kāśī and Avantī]] are easy of access [[(sulabha),|(sulabhā),]] but Ayodhyā, Māyā and Dvārakā are difficult of approach in the Kali age and in Ms. D. C. No. 63 of [[1875-76|1875–76]] it is said at the end that the city is called [[Dvāravati|Dvāravatī]] because it is the way to [[moksa.|mokṣa.]] It is identified by Yule and others with Barake of the Periplus (Ptolemy pp. [[187-188.)|187–188.)]]

[[Dvidevakula-(under Sriparvata)|Dvidevakula—(under Śrīparvata)]] L. I. 92. 158

[[Dvipa-(probably|Dvīpa—(probably]] the island at the mouth of the Ganges) [[Nr. 65.7 .|Nṛ. 65. 7 q.]] by T. K. p. 251 (where [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] is worshipped as Ananta Kapila)

[[Dvīpesvara–(under Narmada)|Dvīpeśvara—(under Narmadā)]] M. 193. 80, P. I. 18. 38 and [[23.76|23. 76.]]

[[Ekadhāra–( under Sabhramati)P.|Ekadhāra—(under Sābhramatī) P.]] VI. 136. 12

[[Ekahamsa-V.|Ekahaṁsa—V.]] 83. 20

[[Ekāmraka-(in|Ekāmraka—(in]] Utkala, Orissa, about 20 miles from Cuttack). This is [[Rudra-tirtha.|Rudra-tīrtha.]] Ekāmraka is the ancient name, the modern one being Bhuwaneśvara. It was also called

[[| Krttivasa.|Kṛttivāsa.]] B (chap. 41. [[10-93 )|10–93)]] describes and glorifies this [[tirtha,|tīrtha,]] all those verses being quoted in T. C. pp. [[176-180.|176–180.]] It is said to be the destroyer of sin, equal to Benares, and as having eight [[sub tirthas|sub-tīrthas]] and it was so called because in former ages there was one mango tree (B. 34. 6 and 41. [[10-93).|10–93).]] Vide Hunter’s ‘Orissa’ vol. I. p. [[231-241|231–241]] and Mitra’s [[‘Antiquties|‘Antiquities]] of Orissa’ vol. II. pp. [[56-98|56–98]] for history, description, daily services and festivals &c. The chief temple is 160 feet high from the base to the top of the [[kalasa.|kalaśa.]] In the [[Bhuvanesvara|Bhuvaneśvara]] Inscription (edited by Dr. L. D. Barnett) in [[E, I, XIIL|E. I. XIII]] p. 150 it is stated that [[Candrika.|Candrikā,]] daughter of the [[Ganga|Gaṅga]] king [[Ananga Bhima|Anaṅga-Bhīma]] and widow of Haihaya prince Paramardin, built a temple of [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] at [[Ekamra.|Ekāmra.]] In that inscription among other matters there is a laudation of Utkala, of the sanctuary of Ekāmra and of the lake Bindusaras (mentioned in B. 41. [[53-54).|53–54).]] The date of the inscription is doubtful. But it is between [[sake|śake]] [[1101-1200.|1101–1200.]] There is a vast number of temples and shrines here. Vide Archaeological survey of India Report for [[1902-3|1902–3]] pp. [[43-44.|43–44,]] [[Purusottamatattva|Puruṣottamatattva]] (Jīv. I. p. 573) where Raghunandana quotes several verses of B. chap. 41. There is Ekāmrapurāṇa in five [[amsas|aṁśas]] (parts) and 70 chapters (vide Mitra’s Notices vol. IV pp. [[138-140|138–140]] No. 1561 for detailed analysis of contents and Ekāmra Candrikā which is a guide to pilgrims visiting [[Bhuvanesvara|Bhuvaneśvara]] and contains extensive quotations from [[Kapilasamhitā, Ekāmrapurana, Sivapurana|Kapilasamhitā, Ekāmrapurāṇa, Śivapurāṇa]] and other works [[vida|vide]] Mitra’s Notices, vol. IV. pp. [[136,197|136–137]] No. 1560 for analysis of [[contents PUNTO|contents).]]

[[Ekavirā—(under Go ) B. 161, 53 min terminar|Ekavīrā—(under Go) B. 161. 1]]

Talegaon copperplates of [[Rāstrakūta|Rāṣṭrakūṭa]] [[Krsnarāja|Kṛṣṇarāja]] I dated [[sake|śake]] 690 [[(768-769|(768–769]] A. [[Girinagara-(modern Junagad|Girinagara—(modern Junāgaḍ]] in Kathiawar). The hill near it was called Ujjayanta or [[Urjayanta|Ūrjayanta]] in former times, but now it is called Girnar. Dey has a long note (pp. 65–66) on it. The pādukās (stone with footprints) of Dattātreya are shown on a spur of the hill. As there are [[Asoka’s|Aśoka’s]] edicts engraved here it follows that it was a well-known place in the third century B. C. The [[Junāgad|Junāgaḍ]] Inscription of Rudradāman (150 A. D.) mentions it in the very first line (E. I. vol. VIII. p. 36 at p 42). Vide under [[Vastrapatha.|Vastrāpatha.]]

[[Girivraja-Capital|Girivraja—Capital]] of the kings of Magadha from Jarāsandha and his son Sahadeva; called [[Rājagrha|Rājagṛha]] in Buddhist times. It is about 62 miles from Patna. Dey has a very long note on it (pp. 66–69); S. 21. [[2-3|2–3]] (it was surrounded and guarded by five hills called Vaihāra, Vipula, Varāha, [[Vrsabha,|Vṛṣabha,]] [[Rsigiri).|Ṛṣigiri).]] Vide under [[Rājagrha.|Rājagṛha.]] Rām. I. 32. 7 states that it was founded by Vasu, a son of [[Kusa,|Kuśa,]] son of Brahmā.

[[Haramunda-(a|Haramuṇḍa—(a]] [[tirtha|tīrtha]] near [[Kasmira|Kāśmīra)]] NM 1455

[[Haridvara-(same|Haridvāra—(same]] as [[Gangadvara|Gaṅgādvāra]] and [[Māyāpuri)|Māyāpurī)]] It is in the modern Saharanpur District in U. P. and on the right bank of the Ganges. It is one of the seven holy cities (vide pp. 501 and 678) P. IV. 17. 66, VI. 21. 1, VI. 22. 18, VI. 135. 37 ([[Māndavya|Māṇḍavya]] practised penance here). See Beal’s BRWW vol I. p. 197, where Hiouen Thsang states that men of the five Indies call it the Gate of [[Gangā|Gaṅgā]] and that hundreds and thousands of people gather to bathe and wash. I do not agree with Cunningham (A. G. p. 353) that Haridvāra is a comparatively modern name, since Alberuni mentions only [[Gangādvāra.|Gaṅgādvāra.]] Both SK. IV and P. IV mention Haridvāra and it cannot be said that they both are later than Alberuni (i. e. 1030 A. D.). Probably [[Gangādvāra|Gaṅgādvāra]] was a more popular name in the 11th century than Haridvāra. Al. (vol. I. p. 199) tells us that the source of the Ganges is called [[Gangādvāra.|Gaṅgādvāra.]]

[[Hemakūta-(another|Hemakūṭa—(another]] name of [[Kailasa,|Kailāsa,]] which see) [[Bhi.|Bhī.]] 6, 4, Br. II. 14. 48 and 15. 15 (Himavat and [[Hemakuta|Hemakūṭa]] distinguished)

[[Hetukeśvara–(under|Hetukeśvara—(under]] VS) L. q. by T. K. 92

[[Himalaya-see|Himālaya—see]] Himavat

[[Himavat-In|Himavat—In]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 121. 4 and A. V. IV. 2. 5 the plural is used ([[Visve|Viśve]] [[himavantah).|himavantaḥ).]] But in A. V. V. 4. 2 and 8, VI. 24. 1 the singular is employed. In [[Kenopanisad|Kenopaniṣad]] III. 25 Umā [[Haimavati|Haimavatī]] is mentioned. In V. 158. 19, Udyoga 11. 12 and [[Pan.|Pāṇ.]] IV. 4. 112 Himavat is mentioned and in K. II, 37. [[46-49|46–49]] its length is given as 1080 yojanas. It is the [[Varsaparvata|Varṣaparvata]] of [[Bharatavarsa|Bhāratavarṣa]] and the other seven chief mountains mentioned in note 1260 are ‘Kulaparvatas’. M. 117–118 contain fine descriptions of trees, flowers, birds and beasts on it. [[Himalaya|Himālaya]] occurs in non-vedic works, e. g. Gītā X. 25. Himavat meant the whole mountain range stretching from Assam in the east to the mountains west of Panjab. Mār. 51. 24 states that Kailāsa and Himavat stretch from east to west and are situated between two seas and that Himavat is to the north of [[Bharatavarsa|Bhāratavarṣa]] (which has seas on south, west, and east) like the string of a bow (Mār. 54. 59)

I. p. 186) regards Erannoboas and Sonos as distinct. It was called ‘golden armed’ probably owing to the colour of the sand in its bed or because particles of gold were found in it.

[[Kārtikeyapada-|Kārtikeyapada—]](in [[Gaya|Gayā]]) Vā 109. 19, 111. 54

[[Kasi–|Kāśī—]]Vide pp. 618-642 above. It is probably the Kassida of Ptolemy (p. 228). AC (p. 182) notes that [[Kasi Varānasi, Vārānasi and Sivapuri|Kāśī Vārāṇasī, Varāṇasī and Śivapurī]] are synonyms (verse 974 )

[[Kaśmira-mandala–|Kaśmīra-maṇḍala—]]The ancient name seems to have been [[Kaśmira.|Kaśmīra.]] The Mbh [[(vol. II. p. 119 )|(vol. II. p. 119)]] on [[Pan.|Pāṇ.]] III. 2. 114 employs this form [[(abhijādasi Devadatta Kasmiran gamiyyamah )|(abhijānāsi Devadatta Kaśmīrān gamiṣyāmaḥ)]]. [[Kaśmira|Kaśmīra]] as the name of a country occurs in the [[Sindhvadigana.

(Pan.|Siṃdhvādigaṇa

(Pāṇ.]] IV. 3. 93). In NM the country is called [[Kasmira|Kaśmīra]] in many verses such as 5, 11, 43, 50, but sometimes [[Kasmira also.|Kāśmīra also.]] Later works also like H. C. 14.45 employ the form [[Kasmira;|Kaśmīra ;]] the [[Vik (18. 1 and 18 )|Vik (18. 1 and 18)]] uses the form [[Kasmira.|Kāśmīra.]] NM (292-93) derives the name from ka meaning water [[( kam vāri Harina yasmād-desad-asmad-apākrtam | Kas mirākhyam tato hyasya nama loke bhavisyati)|(kaṃ vāri Hariṇā yasmād-deśād-asmād-apākṛtam । Kaś- mīrākhyaṃ tato hyasya nāma loke bhaviṣyati ॥)]]. Ptolemy speaks of it as Kaspeiria and states that the country was situated below the sources of Bidaspes [[(Vitastā). Sandabal (Can drabhāga) and Adris (Iravati).|(Vitastā), Sandabal (Can- drabhāgā) and Adris (Irāvatī).]] Vide Ptolemy pp. 108-109 and NM 43. The Vanaparva speaks of the whole country of [[Kasmira as boly|Kāśmīra as holy]] (130. 10). Ain. A. (vol. II p. 354 notes that the whole of [[Kaśmira|Kaśmīra]] is regarded as holy ground. Vide also V 82. 90, S. 27. 17, Anu 25. 8. According to a treaty in 1846 with the Maharaja of Kashmir and Jammu the territories of the [[Maha raja are situated|Mahā- raja are ‘situated]] to the eastward of the river Indus and westward of the river [[Rāvi’|Rāvī’]] I. G. I. vol. XV p. 72. The Kashmir [[yalley|valley]] is approximately [[84 miles.comin length|84 miles in length]] and 20 to 25 [[mill-inbreadth (I. G. I. vol. XV PFOA y|miles in breadth (I. G. I. vol. XV p. 100).]] Stein (SM p. 63 ) says that [[it extent is confined to the greathamallexendrained|its extent is confined to the great valley drained]]

[[P766]]by the head waters of the Vitastā and to the minor slopes of [[moun tains|moun- tains]] that surround it. Hiouen Thsang [[(Beal’s B RW W|(Beal’s B R W W]] vol. 1. p. 148) states that [[‘Kasmīra|‘Kaśmīra]] kingdom is about 7000 li (one li being equal to about [[5 miles),|5 1/2 miles),]] that as the country is protected by a dragon it has always assumed superiority among neighbouring people, that they love learning and are well instructed and that [[(p. 149 )|(p. 149)]] the country was once a dragon lake and that afterwards it became dry’. The words of Hiouen Thsang refer to an ancient legend according to which Kashmir was originally a lake [[(called Satisaras)|(called Satīsaras)]] 6 yojanas long and 3 yojanas wide and later became [[Satidesa|Satīdeśa]] [[(NM 64-66). that|(NM 64-66), that]] Umā herself is the country of [[Kasmira|Kaśmīra]] (NM 31), that the divine Vitastā rising in the [[Himalayas|Himālayas]] is like the [[simanta|sīmanta]] (line parting the hair) of this holy land (NM 45). The legend runs that [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] conferred a boon on [[Vasuki-nāga|Vāsuki-nāga]] who prayed to Viṣṇu when [[Garuda|Garuḍa]] began to devour all nāgas that he should stay in the [[Satidesa|Satīdeśa]] along with the other nāgas, that no enemy would kill any nāga (NM 105-107) and that [[Nila|Nīla]] was made king of nāgas in the [[Satidesa (NM 110).|Satīdeśa (NM 110).]] The residence of [[Nila|Nīla]] is the famous fountain near the village of [[Verrāg|Vērnāg]] in the [[Shababad|Shahabad]] pargana. A certain demon called Jalodbhava grew in the [[Sati|Satī]] lake and killed men (NM 111-123 and Vām. 81. 30-33). Nīla approached the sage [[Kasyapa|Kaśyapa]] the father of all Nāgas, at whose intercession [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] asked Anantanāga to pierce the hills and make the lake dry and then killed the demon Jalodbhava (R. I. 25). Then [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] asked the Nāgas to live in peace and harmony with men. [[Sati|Satī]] became the river Vitastā. Vide Kūrma II. 43. 4. Nāga in Kashmir means the tutelary [[d eity|deity]] presiding over the several holy springs, pools and lakes in which [[Kasmira|Kaśmīra]] abounds. Both NM [[(1130 31)|(1130- 31)]] and R [[(I. 38 )|(I. 38)]] state that there is not a spot in [[Kasmira|Kaśmīra]] even as small as a grain of sesamum which is not a [[tirtha|tīrtha]] and has not a nāga as its presiding deity. In [[Ain. A (vol. II. p. 354 )|Ain. A (vol. II. p. 354)]] Abul Fazl notes that there were in his day 45 shrines of [[Mahadeva,|Mahādeva,]] 64 of [[Visnu,|Viṣṇu,]] 3 of [[Brahma|Brahmā]] and 22 of Durgā and that in 700 places there were graven images of snakes which were worshipped and about which wonderful stories were told. R [[(1.72)|(I. 72)]] and NM (313-314) say that the country of [[Kasmira is Parvati,|Kaśmīra is Pārvatī,]] that the king thereof should be deemed to be a part of [[Siva|Śiva]] and that none who desires prosperity should disobey [[(or disre spect)|(or disre- spect)]] the king. R (I. 42) summarises in one verse the peculiar features of [[Kaśmira|Kaśmīra]] viz. ’learning, lofty dwellings, saffron, water containing ice and grapes; these are common here, although rare in the three worlds’.

18,|Vā. 109. 18,]] 111. 49 and 58

[[Konarka or Konaditya-(in Odra ie.|Koṇārka or Koṇāditya—(in Oḍra i. e.]] Orissa, about 24 miles north- west of [[Jagannatha Puri).|Jagannātha Purī).]] It means ’the sun of [[Kona’. Konākona|Koṇa’. Koṇākoṇa]] appears to have been the ancient name. It is a most exquisite memorial of sun-worship. It con- tains magnificent ruins of a temple of the Sun, which was built by the [[Ganga|Gaṅga]] king [[Narasimhadeva|Narasiṃhadeva]] [[(1238 1264 A.D.).|(1238- 1264 A. D.).]] It is a glorious and most beautiful example of north Indian Hindu architecture ever erected. It had a tower over 180 feet in height and a porch [[(mandapa)|(maṇḍapa)]] in front of 140 feet high. See Mitra’s ‘Anti- quities of Orissa’ vol. II pp. 145-156 and Hunter’s ‘Orissa’ vol. I. p. 288 and [[‘Sungod of Konārka|‘Sungod of Koṇārka]] unearthed’ (with illustrations) in Modern Review for 1945 pp. 67-72. The Sun temple shows some of the finest animal sculptures executed in India. Vide [[B.28, verses 2,9.|B. 28, verses 2, 9,]] 11, 47, 65 and 29.1 and T. C. pp. 180 ff. It is probably the Kannagara of Ptolemy (vide Ptolemy p. 70)

[[Lonārakunda–(in Visnu-Gayā) P. VI. 176.41. Lonār is a salt lake in the Buldana District of Berar, the fabled den of the giant Lonāsura whom Visnu vanquished. It is a place of great antiquity and held in great veneration. Ain. A. vol. II. describes it (pp. 230-231) and states that brahmanas call it Bishan Gayā. One of the best remaining examples in Berar of the medieval temples is that at Lonār known as the temple of Daitya sūdana. It is a Vaisnava shrine. Vide Victor Cousins’ Medieval temples of the Dakhan’ (1931) pp. 68-72 for description of the great temple and of several temples around the margin of a lake in an extinct volcanic crater and plates CIII-CV.|Loṇārakuṇḍa—(in Viṣṇu-Gayā) P. VI. 176. 41. Loṇār is a salt lake in the Buldana District of Berar, the fabled den of the giant Loṇāsura whom Viṣṇu vanquished. It is a place of great antiquity and held in great veneration. Ain. A. vol. II. describes it (pp. 230-231) and states that brāhmaṇas call it Bishan Gayā. One of the best remainin g examples in Berar of the medieval temples is that at Loṇār known as the temple of Daityasūdana. It is a Vaiṣṇava shrine. Vide Victor Cousins’ ‘Medieval temples of the Dakhan’ (1931) pp. 68-72 for description of the great temple and of several temples around the margin of a lake in an extinct volcanic crater and plates CIII-CV.]]

[[Mārtanda (temple of the Sun in Kasmira) Modern Martan or Matan, 5 miles to north-east of Islamabad. Its popular name is ‘Bavan’ (Bhavana). It overlooks the finest view in Kasmira. [[‘Naimisyā vai sattram-āsata’; while in Pancavimsa Br. 25. 6, 4 occurs Naimisiya and Kausitaki Br. 26. 5 has Naimisiyānām’ and 28. 4 also. II. 20.34, K. II. 43. 1-16 (very dear to Mahādeva). vā 2.8, Br, I. 2.8 (bo‘h derive it as bhramato dharmacakrasya yatra nemir-asiryata’ from ’nemi’ rim of a wheel and śr. to be shattered’), B 1,3-10 (Gne description): Vāyu (I. 14-15) makes it clear that the great sattra of the sages of Naimisaranya was in Kuruksetra on the Drsadvati. But Vā. 2,9 and Br. I. 2.9 say that it was on the Gomati. It is possible that Gomati is only an adjective, It was here that enmity between Vasistha and Visvāmitra flourished, Kalmāṣapāda was cursed by Sakti and Parāśara was born. Vi. [[P794]]kara yields inexhaustible results. This is one (western) of the five [[Vedis|Vedīs]] of Brahmā (P. V. 15. 150). Vām. 22. 19, Br. V. 19. 37). P. V. 15. 63 and 82 explain how this place was called [[Puskara|Puṣkara]] from the lotus [[(puskara)|(puṣkara)]] that Brahmā cast here. Br. (III. 34. 7 ff) states that the hero [[Parasurāma|Paraśurāma]] practised tapas here along with his disciple [[Akrtavrana|Akṛtavraṇa]] for 100 years. The T. K. pp. 182-185 quote verses 20-39 of Vanaparva chap. 82 and twelve verses from P. V. 27 relating to [[Puskara.|Puṣkara.]] Al. (vol. II. p. 147) states: ‘Outside the town in three places they have constructed ponds which stand in high veneration and are places of worship’. The principal temples are five, but all of them are modern, the earlier ones having been destroyed by Aurangzeb. There are several [[sub-tirthas|sub-tīrthas]] under it (V. 82). A—The river starts from [[Brahmasaras, acc. to Salya 51. 19,|Brahmasaras, acc. to Śalya 51. 19,]] from [[Badarikāsrama|Badarikāśrama]] acc. to [[Vām. 2.42-43|Vām. 2. 42-43]] and from [[Plaksa|Plakṣa]] tree acc. to Vām. 32. 3-4, P. V. 18. 159-160 (say that [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] was asked to cast the [[Vādava|Vāḍava]] fire into the sea in the west). This is probably an allusion to volcanic action whereby [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] disappeared. Vām. 3. 8 says that as [[Sankara|Śaṅkara]] guilty of Brahmahatyā plunged into it, it disappeared, while elsewhere (e g. in V. 130. 3-4) it is said that it disappeared through fear of the touch of [[sūdras,|śūdras,]] [[Nisādas|Niṣādas]] and [[Abhiras|Ābhīras]] and Anu. (155. 25-27) states that [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] went to [[Marudesa|Marudeśa]] owing to curse by Utathya and became dry and unholy. After disappearing it becomes visible at Camasodbheda, [[Sivodbheda|Śivodbheda]] and Nāgodbheda. The [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] in [[Kuruksetra|Kurukṣetra]] was called [[prāci|prācī]] [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] (P. V. 18. 181-182). See Dey pp. 180-181 for several [[Sarasvatis.|Sarasvatīs.]] V 130. 1-2 state that those who die on the [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] go to heaven and this was the blessing conferred by [[Daksa|Dakṣa]] when he performed a sacrifice there. Vide Oldham’s paper in JRAS for 1893 pp. 49-76; B—There is another holy river of the same name rising in the south-west end of Aravali hills and flowing south-west through the districts of Palanpur, Mahikantha and Baroda and past the ancient cities of Anhilvād and Siddhapur and falling into the lesser Runn of Cutch. Vide under Prabhāsa. [[Sarasvati-Arunā-sangama-V.|Sarasvatī-Aruṇā-saṅgama—V.]] 83. 151, K II. 30. 22, [[Sal.|Śal.]] 43. 31 and chap. 44. [[Sarasvatipatana-(under Mathura)|Sarasvatīpatana—(under Mathurā)]] Var. 154. 20. [[Sarasvati-sāgara-sangama-V.|Sarasvatī-sāgara-saṅgama—V.]] 82. 60, P. I. 24. 9, Vām. 84. 29. [[Sarāvati-(Probably Rāpti|Śarāvatī—(Probably Rāpti]] in Oudh) [[Bhi. 9. 20;|Bhī. 9. 20;]] [[Pān. VI.3.120 (Sarādinām|Pāṇ. VI. 3. 120 (Śarādīnāṃ]] ca) knew the river [[Sarāvati;|Śarāvatī;]] [[Kșira svāmin (in com. on Amara ‘Sarava tyāstu yosvadheh’)|Kṣīrasvāmin (in com. on Amara ‘Śarāvatyāstu yogvadheḥ’)]] quotes a verse [[‘prāgudancau vibhajate hamsah ksiro dake yathā vidusām sabdasiddh yartham sa nah pātu Saravati|‘prāgudañcau vibhajate haṃsaḥ kṣīrodake yathā | viduṣāṃ śabdasiddhyarthaṃ sā naḥ pātu Śarāvatī]] [[Rr. Agrawala in (J.U.P.H.R.S.[vok: 1||| Dr. Agrawala in (J. U. P. H. R. S. vol. 16]] p. 15 surmises that it is the [[Ghag ghar|Ghagghar]] flowing through Ambala District. I have grave doubts about

[[P803]]this. It is possible that when the [[Sarasvati|Sarasvatī]] became dried and only a marshy bed was left it was called [[Sarāvati,|Śarāvatī,]] but that in the times of the [[Amarakosa, Sarāvati|Amarakośa, Śarāvatī]] is probably the river [[Sarāvati|Śarāvatī]] which falls into the sea near Honavar (North Kanara District) on which are the famous Gersappa falls. In the [[Raghuvamsa (XV.97) Sarāvati|Raghuvaṃśa (XV. 97) Śarāvatī]] is said to have been the capital of Lava, son of Rāma. [[Sarayū-(r.)|Sarayū—(r.)]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IV. 30. 18, V. 33. 9, X. 64. 9 ([[Sarasvati,|Sarasvatī,]] Sarayu and Sindhu are mentioned [[together ),|together).]] In all these places the word is Sarayu, while in classical Sanskrit it is Sarayū or Sarāyū (M. 22. 19, Vā 45. 94, N II. 75. 71, [[Raghuvamsa|Raghuvaṃśa]] 15. 95 and 100). M. 121. 16-17 and Br. II. 18. 70 show that Sarayū springs from the Mānasa lake situated at the foot of the Vaidyutagiri. Ayodhyā was situated on the Sarayū (Rām. II. 49. 15). Sarayū rises from [[Himalaya|Himālaya]] (Vā 45. 94, M. 114. 21). According to [[Pān. VI. 4. 174 (Dāndi- nāyana- )|Pāṇ. VI. 4. 174 (Dāṇḍināyana—)]] the water of Sarayū was called Sārava ([[Kāsikā|Kāśikā]] says [[‘Sarayvām bhavam sāravam udakam’ ).|‘Sarayvāṃ bhavam sāravam udakam’).]] In the Cullavagga (SBE vol. XX p. 302) it is one of the five great rivers of India and in ‘Questions of [[Milinda ‘|Milinda’]] (SBE vol. 35 p. 171) it is one of ten great rivers, but in both places it is written as Sarabhū. Vide T.P pp. 500, 501 where it is stated that it rises from the left toe of [[Visnu|Viṣṇu]] and is joined by Ghargara. It is the Sarabos of Ptolemy (p. 99). It is also called the Ghogra or Gharghara. [[Sārdūla-a Saivaksetra,|Śārdūla—a Śaivakṣetra,]] according to Bar. S. III. 122. [[Sargabindu-(under Narmada)|Sargabindu—(under Narmadā)]] K. II. 42. 23. [[Sarkarāvartā-(r.)|Śarkarāvartā—(r.)]] Bh. V. 19. 18. [[Sarvahrada-V|Sarvahrada—V]] 85. 39 (uncertain [[loca- tion )|location)]] [[Sarvatirtha-P.|Sarvatīrtha—P.]] II. 92. 4 and 7 ([[Prayāga, Puskara,|Prayāga, Puṣkara,]] [[Sarvatirtha|Sarvatīrtha]] and [[Vārānasi)|Vārāṇasī)]] are the four [[tirthas|tīrthas]] that remove all sins including [[brahma- hatyā,|brahmahatyā).]] [[Sarvatirthesvara-(under|Sarvatīrtheśvara—(under]] VS) SK. It is 58 miles north of Ayodhyā; Rām. VII. 107. 4-7, Vā 88. 200 and A. G. p. 409; but some mss. of [[Raghuvamsa|Raghuvaṃśa]] (XV. 97) state that [[Srāvasti|Śrāvastī]] was the capital of Lava. I. vol. XI. [[Srinagara-A|Śrīnagara—A]] (capital of [[Kāśmira).|Kāśmīra).]] It has a long history. R. I. 104 states that [[Asoka|Aśoka]] built [[Srinagari|Śrīnagarī]] with 96 lakhs of houses. Stein in note on this says that Cunningham (A. G. p. 93) located [[Srinagari|Śrīnagarī]] of [[Asoka|Aśoka]] at the site of the present village Pandrethan ([[Kalhana’s|Kalhaṇa’s]] [[Purānādhis- thāna)|Purāṇādhiṣṭhāna)]] on the right bank of Vitastā three miles above modern [[Srinagar.|Śrīnagar.]] Pandrethan is at the foot of the hill Takhta-i-Sulaiman. Pravarasena erected the shrine of [[Pravaresvara|Pravareśvara]] [[P807]]and Pravarasena II built the new capital in the beginning of the 6th century. Hiouen Thsang speaks of the new city (Pravarapura). Vide Beal’s B. R. W. W. vol. I. pp. [[96.|96,]] 148 and 158 and A. G. pp. 95-96. Ain. A. (vol. II. p. 355) states that Koh-i-Sulaiman is to the east of [[Srinagara|Śrīnagara]]. Al. (vol. I p. 207) notes that [[Addisthan|Addiṣṭhan]] ([[adhisthana|adhiṣṭhāna]] the capital of [[Kasmira|Kāśmīra]]) is built on both banks of the Zhelum. For the Dal lake close to [[Srinagara|Śrīnagara]] and one of the most beautiful spots in the world, vide. I. G. I. vol. XI. pp. 124-125; [[B-a|B—a]] town in Garhwal District on the left bank of the [[Alakananda|Alakanandā]] (U. P. Gazetteer vol. 36 p. 200).

I. 15. [[68-69|68–69]] (same story as in M. 188), Agni 113. 4 ([[Gauri,|Gaurī,]] assuming the form of [[Laksmi|Lakṣmī]] practised penance here). Pargiter (p. 290 [[|n]]) does not correctly interpret Ag.; K. II. 20. 35 ([[Srāddha|Śrāddha]] most efficacious here), II. 37. [[13-14|13–14]] (religious suicide allowed [[bere),|here),]] P. I. 39. 17, VI. 20[[|,]] 15 (a great place for yogins and those practising penance). It is a [[Saktakṣetra|Śāktakṣetra]] acc. to [[Bar, S.|Bār. S.]] III. 124. (pp. [[497-499|497–499]] and [[561-562)|561–562)]] argues that [[Ophie|Ophir]] or Sophir (in the Septuagint translation of the Bible) is the country of [[Sauvirk|Sauvīra]] and not Sūrpāraka as [[man goudscholars|many good scholars]] hold. Ptolemy [[mentionsin|mentions it]] as

[[P810]]Soupara. Some eminent scholars hold that Ophir is Abiria (i. e. [[Abhiras|Ābhīras]]) of Ptolemy (p. 140). Vide JRAS for 1898 pp. 253 ff. for [[dis cussion|discussion]] and JBBRAS vol. 15 p. 273 for a long note on [[Sūrpāraka.|Śūrpāraka.]]

[[Taksasila –(modern|Takṣaśilā—(modern]] [[Taxila)|Taxilā)]] [[Svarga rohana-parva|Svargārohaṇa-parva]] 5. 34, [[Va|Vā.]] 88. [[189-90,|189–90,]] Br. III. 63. [[P826]]

CONCLUDING REMARKS ON TĪRTHAS

At the end of this long list of [[tirthas|tīrthas]] a few words, as promised on p. 722 above, must be said about the attitude that we Indians of the 20th century should adopt towards the [[moun tains,|mountains,]] rivers and other holy places in Bhārata. Modern secular education and the prevailing economic conditions, stresses and trends leave hardly any room for moral and spiritual uplift. We are surrounded on all sides by anxiety, want, misery, hardness of heart and crime. Therefore, the endeavour of all those who have the good of our country at heart must be to cherish all such institutions as [[terd|tend]] to lift the mind out of narrowness and to make it concentrate for some time at least on noble thoughts and aspirations and on detachment from the all-engrossing pursuit of [[money, Pilgrimage|money. Pilgrimage]] is one of such institutions. Those who have faith in pilgrimages as enabling a pilgrim to attain heaven, to collect merit and reach release from [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] should give pilgrimages a new orientation, should see that their charity is not wasted on indolent, [[ignor ant|ignorant]] and vicious priests, should improve the methods of worship at holy places in such a way as will satisfy all hygienic requirements. The priestly class at holy places should [[remem ber|remember]] that in the coming generations they are likely to find their occupation gone, unless they improve themselves, unless they try to be less ignorant and indolent than they are at present and really serve as honest business agents or [[Travel lers’|Travellers’]] Guides. The number of people visiting holy places in the belief of accumulating merit is sure to become less and less, as modern secular education spreads. But it would be a calamity for the moral and spiritual greatness of India if pilgrimages to holy mountains and rivers came to be stopped altogether. I would very strongly recommend to all men, however highly educated, the undertaking of pilgrimages to certain hallowed spots. Our country has recently secured independence and it now rests entirely with us to make or mar the character of the teeming millions of this our land. All things that tend to create in Indians the deep feeling that amidst diversities of physical features, food, dress and habits, all of them [[are-one|are one]] people, that there is no part or district of this vast land which cannot claim to have made some substantial contribution [[unity|unto]] religious and philosophical thought, in abiding literature, in works of art and in holy places, that the destinies of the [[Indi aiutantal|Indians]]

[[P827]]of one part of Bhārata are bound up with the destinies of all others. Frequent visits to distant places in Bhārata, mixing up among people of other parts and understanding their peculiar good points, needs and failings is absolutely necessary if we are to keep our freedom. India is thrice blest in the mountain ranges of the [[Himālaya,which|Himālaya, which]] contains the highest peaks in the world and is full of large and life-giving rivers and of numerous shrines associated with the names of the greatest sages and heroes of antiquity. Every Indian who is proud of the great religious and spiritual heritage of our country must make it a point to devote some part of his time to frequenting holy mountains, rivers and other places of pilgrimage. When we see even from a distance the purity, the whiteness and the serenity of the snow-capped peaks of the Himālaya, the play of delicate and exquisite hues and tints of blue, mauve, and pink when the sun begins to shine on them, a sense of wonder, joy and elevation comes upon us. The glorious view of a peak like Kinchinjunga, which is an unforgettable experience, lifts up the whole life of the onlooker to a higher plane for long. Similarly, the sight of the holy Ganges at night or at sunrise in Haridvāra or on the majestic Ghats in Banaras surely empties our minds of meanness and for the moment at least fills our hearts with a sense of nature’s beauty and with purity, and brings us in tune with the [[Infi nite.|Infinite.]] I may state here that the talk of the conquest of Everest in which some members of European expeditions indulge jars on my ears. This great [[peak’|peak]] has so far vanquished (physically) all those who attempted the conquest.1 But in a figurative sense Everest and other high peaks have been for ages [[conquer ing|conquering]] the hearts of men. Even the members of these expeditions will admit that Everest and its brethren have drawn men to themselves from all parts of the world, made them give up their usual uneventful and placid activities, have evoked in those who attempt the climbing of these giants unbelievable courage, endurance, resourcefulness and self-abnegation and have been instrumental in exhibiting to the world what [[un known|unknown]] powers the human spirit possesses. Therefore, these silent but awe-inspiring peaks should be looked upon as teachers of mankind and not as material things to be conquered and dealt with by human beings as they please.

  1. While these pages were passing [[tbrough|through]] the Press, news was [[broad fast|broadcast]] on the [[scry|very]] morning of the coronation of Queen Elizabeth II [[tbat|that]] Sherpa Tensing Norkay and [[Mr|Mr.]] [[Edmond|Edmund]] Hillary, [[tiro pembers|two members]] of Col. [[Huot’s Brilish|Hunt’s British]] Expedition, had successfully climbed to the top of Mount [[Everest,|Everest.]] ↩︎
